Which entropy for general physical theories?
Paolo Perinotti, Alessandro Tosini, Leonardo Vaglini

Abstract
We address the problem of quantifying the information content of a source for an arbitrary information theory, where the information content is defined in terms of the asymptotic achievable compression rate. The functions that solve this problem in classical and quantum theory are Shannon’s and von Neumann’s entropy, respectively. However, in a general information theory there are three different functions that extend the notion of entropy, and this opens the question as to whether any of them can universally play the role of the quantifier for the information content. Here we answer the question in the negative, by evaluating the information content as well as the various entropic functions in a toy theory called Bilocal Classical Theory.



1
Introduction

The experience of quantum information has suggested that viewing quantum theory as an information theory helps in making its most counterintuitive consequences comprehensible. This perspective
promotes the role of information to a fundamental one in the development of a physical
theory
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
. For example, one might
wonder to what extent the laws of physics depend on the properties of physical systems used as information carriers.
Such an approach to foundations of physics already proved successful both in motivating the use of quantum theory on
operational grounds, and in suggesting how viable generalisations of quantum theory for a post-quantum physics should
look
like
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
.

From the above perspective, it is very natural to introduce in a general theory of physical systems some notion of
entropy at a very fundamental level. Indeed, entropic functions have been identified as quantifiers of information
from a source since the pioneering work by Shannon that paved the way to classical information
theory
[
12
]
. Along this line, von Neumann entropy
[
13
]
was studied in
the realm of quantum information theory as a quantifier of
quantum information

[
14
]
.

The importance of Shannon’s and von Neumann’s entropy is due to classical and quantum source coding theorems, proving
that these two functions represent the achievable compression rates for a classical and a quantum source,
respectively
[
12
,
14
]
.

In order to generalise the quantification of information content to a wider scenario of possible information
theories—i.e. theories of systems and their processings
[
6
,
8
]
—various
authors started from the alternate definitions of Shannon’s and von Neumann’s entropies that refer to state
preparations, measurements and their use in a communication scenario, and, as such, they have a counterpart in a
general information theory
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
.

The first problem that one has to face at this point is that the possible definitions are three, and in general they
lead to three functions that are actually different in the general case
[
17
]
. A second, even more
compelling problem, is that it is unknown whether any of the three entropic functions mentioned above is an exact
quantifier corresponding to the best achievable compression rate of a source. In the light of these questions,
classical and quantum theory are very special and fortunate examples of theories where both problems have a known
solution: both theories are indeed
monoentropic
—i.e. the three entropic functions
coincide
[
15
]
—and the unique entropy exactly matches the information content of a source,
defined as its best achievable compression rate.

In this scenario, one can then ask two crucial and independent questions: i)
what are the features that make a theory monoentropic; ii) under what
conditions is the information content quantified by at least one of the three
possible entropies—or a regularized version of them.

In order to tackle the second question, in Ref.
[
18
]
the present authors set a minimal framework
where the question can be addressed, and they introduced a thorough definition of information content, proving a
lower bound for such quantity in terms of one of the three entropies. The latter result was then used to prove that a
mixed state cannot have null information content, while, on the other hand, under suitable conditions, a pure state
has null information content. In turn, the latter result opened a further question: do there exist theories allowing for pure states that have non-null information content?

In the present paper, we study the above questions in the theory called Bilocal Classical Theory
(BCT)
[
19
]
, and show that, while BCT is monoentropic, the information content of its states
does not coincide
with its entropy. Moreover, in BCT pure states actually have non-null information content. The
latter feature can be understood considering that the independent preparation of two systems in pure
states does not correspond to a pure state for the composite system. Such a preparation thus introduces some ignorance
about the whole system, even if the outcomes of independent experiments on its components are fully predictable. The
latter result can be extended to any theory of classical systems where the rule for composing systems is such that the
composition of pure states is not necessarily pure. Therefore, thinking of pure states as representing complete
knowledge of the physical system is inaccurate if the composition law is not purity-preserving.

2
An overview of the framework

A brief review of the framework follows. A standard reference is
[
5
]
, but an extended treatment can be found in
[
8
]
. The framework is also carefully reviewed in the introduction of
[
19
]

2.1
Review of the framework

The primitive notions of an operational theory are those of test, event and system. A
test

\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}

is given by a collection of
events
, where
i
labels the elements
of the
outcome space

\mathsf{X}
. The
systems
(or, more precisely,
system types
)
allow for the connection between different tests, and are denoted by capital Roman letters
\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\dots
. Therefore,
a test is completely determined by its input and output systems, and the events associated with the outcome space
\mathsf{X}
.
In order to represent a test and its events
\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
we use the usual diagrammatic notation

,

and we will call
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{B}
the input and the output system of the test, respectively. In this case we will say that
the test
\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
, as well as each of its events
\mathscr{A}_{i}
, are of type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
.
If
\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
and
\{\mathscr{B}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}
are two tests,
one can define their
sequential composition
as the test
\{\mathscr{C}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}
, with events

\mathscr{C}_{i,j}

Notice that this definition requires the output system of the events on the left to be necessarily the input system of
the events on the right. In formula, the sequential composition will be denote as
\mathscr{C}_{i,j}\coloneqq\mathscr{B}_{j}\mathscr{A}_{i}
. A
singleton test
is a test whose outcome space set
\mathsf{X}
is a singleton, and the
unique event contained in it is called
deterministic
. For any system
\mathrm{A}
there exists a unique

identity
test
\{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}}\}
such that, for any event of type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
, one has
\mathscr{A}\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}
. Another operation that can be performed on tests for defining a new test is
parallel
composition
. Given two systems
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
we call
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
the composite system of
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
. Then, if

\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
and
\{\mathscr{C}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}
are two tests of type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
and
\mathrm{C}\to\mathrm{D}
, respectively, their
parallel composition is the test
\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}_{j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}
. Diagrammatically

Both the parallel and sequential compositions must be associative,
and the parallel composition operation commutes with the sequential one, namely

(\mathscr{E}_{h}\boxtimes\mathscr{F}_{k})(\mathscr{C}_{i}\boxtimes\mathscr{D}_{j})=(\mathscr{E}_{h}\mathscr{C}_{i})\boxtimes(\mathscr{F}_{k}\mathscr{D}_{j})
.
The identity
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
of the composite system
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
is the parallel composition of the two identities
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{B}}

(1)

We will denote by
\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes k}
the composition
of the same system
\mathrm{A}
with itself
k
times.

There is a special kind of system, the
trivial system
I, satisfying
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{I}\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{A}
for every system
\mathrm{A}
.
Tests with
\mathrm{I}
as input system and
\mathrm{A}
as the output one are called
preparation tests
of
\mathrm{A}
, while tests
with input system
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{I}
as output are named
observation tests
of
\mathrm{A}
. The events of a preparation
test
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
and of an observation test
\{a_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}
are represented through the following
diagrams

In the following we will always use Greek letters to denote preparation tests and Latin letters for the observation
ones. Preparation and observation events will also be denoted by using round brackets:
|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}
and

(a|_{\mathrm{A}}
, respectively, and the system will be omitted whenever it is clear from the context.

A
circuit
is a diagram representing an arbitrary test that is obtained by sequential and parallel composition of other tests. We say that a circuit is
closed
when the input and output systems are both the trivial one, namely, when it starts with a preparation test
and it ends with an observation test. For any pair of systems, we want their agents to be allowed to exchange their systems. This requirement is captured by the notion of
braiding
, that is
a family of reversible transformations
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
, defined for any pair of systems of the theory, and denoted as follows

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ .

(3)

Generally,
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}^{-1}
are different transformations. When they are equal, e.g. in the case of QT, where
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
is represented by the swap operator,
the theory is called symmetric. These transformations must obey a sliding property, which asserts that two agents can equivalently perform their transformations and then exchange their
output systems, or first exchange their input systems and then perform the transformations. In diagrams

(4)

Then,
an
operational Theory
is defined by a collection of systems, that is closed under composition, and a collection of tests (including all the family of
tests
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
for any
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
) closed under a pair of associative operations of sequential and parallel composition.

An
operational
probabilistic theory
is an operational theory where the test corresponding to any closed
circuit (equivalently, any test from the trivial system to itself) is given by a joint probability distribution
for its outcomes, conditioned by the tests that make the circuit. Moreover, compound tests from the trivial system to
istelf are independent, namely, the joint probability distribution is assumed to be given by the product of the probability
distributions of the composing tests. A simple example is given by a preparation test
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}

sequentially followed by an observation test
\{a_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}
:

\displaystyle\{p(i,j|\{\rho_{l}\},\{a_{m}\})\}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.74971pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-6.74971pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\rho_{i}\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\rho_{i}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\rho_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\rho_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\rho_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\rho_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.00002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.75pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.77438pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.49945pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-7.24973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}a_{j}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.49945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.72223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{a_{j}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.49945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{a_{j}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.49945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{a_{j}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.49945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{a_{j}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.49945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{a_{j}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\right\},

with
\sum_{i,j}p(i,j)=1
. Thus, one has a joint probability distribution, which is conditioned by the chosen tests

\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
and
\{a_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}
. From now on we will simply omit this dependence.
The probability associated with the closed circuit where a preparation
\rho_{i}
is followed by an observation
a_{j}

will also be denoted by a pairing,
p(i,j)=(a_{j}|\rho_{i})
.

Given any system
\mathrm{A}
of an OPT, One can define an equivalence relation on the set of preparation events by declaring
that
\rho\sim\sigma
iff
(a|\rho)=(a|\sigma)
for any observation event
a
. The set of
equivalence classes with respect to this relation is called the set of
states
of system
\mathrm{A}
and it is denoted
by
\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
. Similarly, one can define the set of
effects
as the set of equivalence classes of the
observation events such that
(a|\rho)=(b|\rho)
for any preparation event
\rho
, and this is
denoted by
\mathsf{Eff}(\mathrm{A})
. The sets of deterministic states and effects, obtained as the equivalence classes of deterministic preparation events and deterministic observation events respectively, will be denoted by
\mathsf{St}_{1}({\mathrm{A}})
and

\mathsf{Eff}_{1}({\mathrm{A}})
respectively. By definition, effects are separating for states, namely
|\rho)\neq|\sigma)
,
iff there exists an effect
(a|
such that
(a|\rho)\neq(a|\sigma)
, and viceversa states are separating for
effects.

Given the probabilistic structure, states can be seen as functionals on the set of effects and viceversa, and then one
can consider linear combinations of them, thus defining two linear spaces,
\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathsf{Eff}(\mathrm{A})_{\mathbb{R}}
, which
are dual to each other—assuming that they are finite-dimensional. The
size

D_{\mathrm{A}}
of a given system
\mathrm{A}

is defined as the dimension of the linear space
\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{A})
.
Also the set of events of generic type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
can be endowed with an equivalence relation. Indeed, given
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
of the
same type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
, we say that they are
operationally equivalent
(
\mathscr{A}\sim\mathscr{B}
) if the following identity
holds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern =\ \begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.8889pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.8889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 4.49997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.8889pt\raise-18.99995pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.88896pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.5417pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-6.5417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt\raise-3.41666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{B}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.88899pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.54167pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{B}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.88902pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.00002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.88904pt\raise-7.24998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.41666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.38905pt\raise 4.49997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.38905pt\raise-18.99995pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-8.8889pt\raise-14.49998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\Psi}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 16.88893pt\raise-14.49998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.88896pt\raise-14.49998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.61111pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{C}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.88899pt\raise-14.49998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.88902pt\raise-14.49998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.00002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\ ,

for any preparation event
\Psi
, observation event
A
and any ancillary system
C
. We then denote the set of all the equivalence classes with
\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})
, whose elements are simply called
transformations
. The operations of sequential and parallel composition are then extended to the equivalence classes in the obvious way, by means of the representatives. For instance, if
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
are two transformation events of type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
and
\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{C}
respectively, then the sequential composition between the equivalence classes is defined as
[\mathscr{A}][\mathscr{B}]\coloneqq[\mathscr{A}\mathscr{B}]
. From now on we will always refer to transformations omitting the square bracket in the notation. Via the sequential composition, a transformation of type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}
is associated with a map from
\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{C})
to
\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C})
for any ancillary system
\mathrm{C}
, which can be uniquely extended to linear maps between the vector spaces
\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{C})
to
\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C})
(see
[
5
]
). Therefore, a transformations is completely characterised by a family of linear maps, one for each ancillary system
\mathrm{C}
.
As for states and effects, the set of deterministic transformations will be denoted by
\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})
.
If
\mathscr{U}\in\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})
and there exists
\mathscr{V}\in\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A})
such that
\mathscr{V}\mathscr{U}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{B}}
, we say that
\mathscr{U}
is
reversible
.
Accordingly, two systems
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
are called
operationally equivalent
(
\mathrm{A}\cong\mathrm{B}
) if there exists a reversible
transformation
\mathscr{U}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})
. A notion that will be useful in the following is that of
asymptotically
equivalent systems
.

Definition 2.1

(Asymptotic equivalence)
.

Two systems
\mathrm{A}_{1}
and
\mathrm{A}_{2}
are asymptotically equivalent if the following conditions are satisfied

1.

there exists a pair of integers
k_{1},k_{2}<\infty
,
\mathscr{E}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}\to\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}})
and
\mathscr{D}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}}\to\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}})
such that
\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}}
;

2.

there exists a pair of integers
h_{1},h_{2}<\infty
,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes h_{2}}\to\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes h_{1}})
and
\mathscr{F}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes h_{1}}\to\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes h_{2}})
such that
\mathscr{F}\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes h_{2}}}
;

3.

let
M_{2}^{\rm min}(k_{1})
be the smallest
k_{2}
such that item
1
is satisfied for a given
k_{1}
, and similarly for

M_{1}^{\rm min}(h_{2})
with reference to item
2
. The following relations hold:

\lim_{k_{1}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M_{2}^{\rm min}(k_{1})}{k_{1}}=k,\quad\lim_{h_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M_{1}^{\rm min}(h_{2})}{h_{2}}=k^{-1}.

(5)

Notice that item
1
in the above definition is equivalent to the following: for any
k_{1}<\infty
there exists
k_{2}<\infty
and a pair of maps
\mathscr{E}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}\to\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}})
and
\mathscr{D}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}}\to\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}})
such that
\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}}
.
Indeed, the above statement implies item
1
, as we now prove.

Assuming the validity of item 1 in definition 2.1, if
k=k_{1}
, the statement is trivially true. We then first prove that for every integer
k<k_{1}
there exists another integer
k_{2}
and a pair of maps
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}
and
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}
such that
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}\tilde{\mathscr{E}}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}}
. Let
\mathscr{E}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}\to\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}})
and
\mathscr{D}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}}\to\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}})
be the maps given by item 1, such that

\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}}
. Let
|\rho)\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes(k_{1}-k)})
and define the map
\mathscr{E}_{\rho}
as the sequential composition of the encoding map
\mathscr{E}
, preceeded by the parallel composition of
|\rho)
—which is a map from the trivial system
\mathrm{I}
to
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes(k_{1}-k)}
—with the identity
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes k}}
on the remaining
k
systems, i.e.,

\mathscr{E}_{\rho}\coloneqq\mathscr{E}\circ(|\rho)\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}})\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}\to\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}}).

Similarly we do for the decoding: we take the decoding
\mathscr{D}
and a deterministic effect
(e|\in\mathsf{Eff}_{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes(k_{1}-k)})
(i.e. a map from
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes(k_{1}-k)}
to the trivial system) and we define the sequential composition of
\mathscr{D}
with
(e|\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}}
, to obtain

\mathscr{D}_{e}\coloneqq((e|\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}})\circ\mathscr{D}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}}\to\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}).

It is now clear that
\mathscr{D}_{e}\mathscr{E}_{\rho}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}}
, indeed, using the fact that parallel and sequential compositions commute

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{e}\mathscr{E}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=[((e|\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}})\circ\mathscr{D}]\circ[\mathscr{E}\circ(|\rho)\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}})]

\displaystyle=((e|\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}})(|\rho)\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}})

\displaystyle=(e|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes(k_{1}-k)}}\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}}

where the last step follows from the fact that we have chosen a deterministic state
|\rho)
and a deterministic effect
(e|
to define the encoding
\mathscr{E}_{\rho}
and the decoding
\mathscr{D}_{e}
respectively, so that
(e|\rho)=1
. Now, for
k>k_{1}
, let
m
be the integer such that
(m-1)k_{1}\leq k<mk_{1}
and let
\tilde{k}\coloneqq mk_{1}-k\leq k_{1}
. We prove that there exists an integer
\tilde{k}_{2}
and a pair
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}\to\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}})
and
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}
that perfectly encodes
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}
onto
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes k_{2}}
, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}\tilde{\mathscr{E}}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}}
. For
\tilde{k}\leq k_{1}
we have seen that there exists a pair
(\tilde{\mathscr{E}},\tilde{\mathscr{D}})
such that
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}\tilde{\mathscr{E}}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes(k_{1}-\tilde{k})}}
. Then consider the encoding map from
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}
to
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\boxtimes mk_{2}}
defined as the composition of
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}
with
m-1
copies of
\mathscr{E}
, say
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}\boxtimes\mathscr{E}^{\boxtimes(m-1)k_{1}}
. Similarly for the decoding, take
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}\boxtimes\mathscr{D}^{\boxtimes(m-1)k_{1}}
. It is now clear that this pair gives a perfect encoding of
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\boxtimes k}
onto
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{mk_{2}}
. This ensures that the statement in item 3 of definition
2.1
definitely makes sense.

Now we set up some terminology and we introduce pure and mixed states, as well as the definition of state dilation.

Definition 2.2

(Refinement and convex refinement of an event)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}\in\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})
.

1.

A refinement of
\mathscr{C}
is given by a
collection of events
\{\mathscr{B}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}\subseteq\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})

such that there exists a test
\{\mathscr{B}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
with
\mathsf{Y}\subseteq\mathsf{X}
and

\mathscr{C}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}\mathscr{B}_{j}
.
We say that a refinement
\{\mathscr{D}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{Y}}
is trivial if
\mathscr{D}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\mathscr{C}
,
\lambda_{i}\in[0,1]

for every
i\in\mathsf{Y}
. Conversely,
\mathscr{C}
is called the coarse-graining of the events
\{\mathscr{D}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{Y}}
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{C})
the collection of
all the refinements of
\mathscr{C}
.

2.

A convex refinement or decomposition of
\mathscr{C}
is given by a collection of pairs
\{(p_{j},\mathscr{B}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B})
where
\{p_{j}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}
is a probability distribution and
\mathscr{B}_{j}
are events,
such that there exist tests
\{\mathscr{B}^{(j)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
, with
\mathscr{B}^{(j)}_{i_{0}}=\mathscr{B}_{j}
for all
j\in\mathsf{Y}
, for which the collection
\{p_{j}\mathscr{B}^{(j)}_{i}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}
is a legitimate conditional test, and
\mathscr{C}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}p_{j}\mathscr{B}_{j}
. We say that a convex refinement is trivial if
\mathscr{B}_{j}=\mathscr{C}
for any
j\in\mathsf{Y}
.

Definition 2.3

(Atomic, refinable and extremal events)
.

An event
\mathscr{C}
is called atomic if it admits only trivial refinements, and refinable if it is not atomic.
\mathscr{C}
is called extremal if it admits
only trivial convex refinements.

The notion of refinement and refinable events give rise to the definitions of
pure
and
mixed
states.

Definition 2.4

(Pure and mixed states)
.

\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
is called pure if it is extremal and deterministic. We will denote by
\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A})
the set of all the pure
states of system
\mathrm{A}
. On the other hand,
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
is called mixed if it is
neither atomic nor extremal.

Given any state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
, any convex decomposition made of pure states will be accordingly called pure. The set of pure convex decompositions
of
\rho
will be denoted by
\mathcal{P}(\rho)=\{\{(p_{i},\phi_{i})\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}|\sum_{i}p_{i}\phi_{i}=\rho,\;\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}\subseteq\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}),\;\sum_{i}p_{i}=1\}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
and
\Psi\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})
. We say that
\Psi
is a dilation of
\rho
if there exists a deterministic effect
e\in\mathsf{Eff}_{1}(\mathrm{B})
such that

\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern =\ \begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern .

If
\Psi
is also pure, then we say
that it is a purification of
\rho
and
\mathrm{B}
is called the purifying system. We denote by
D_{\rho}
the set of all dilations of the state
\rho
, and by
P_{\rho}
the set of all its purifications.

Trivially one has that
P_{\rho}\subseteq D_{\rho}
. Moreover, if
\Omega\in D_{\rho}
, then one has
D_{\Omega}\subseteq D_{\rho}

The linear space
\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{A})
can be endowed with a metric structure by means of the following norm,
which has an operational
meaning related to optimal discrimination schemes
[
8
]
.

Definition 2.6

(Operational norm)
.

The norm of an element
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})_{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as

\lVert\rho\rVert_{\rm op}\coloneqq\sup_{\{a_{0},a_{1}\}\subseteq\mathsf{Eff}(\mathrm{A})}(a_{0}-a_{1}|\rho),

where
\{a_{0},a_{1}\}
is any binary observation test.

This norm reduces to the usual trace-norm in the case of quantum theory. Moreover, it satisfies a monotonicity property, as stated in the following lemma
(see
[
8
]
for the proof).

Lemma 2.1

(Monotonicity of the operational norm)
.

For any
\delta\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{A})
and
\mathscr{C}\in\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B})
the following inequality holds

\lVert\mathscr{C}\delta\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lVert\delta\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},

(6)

with equality holding iff
\mathscr{C}
is reversible.

An OPT can enjoy different degrees of locality according to the following definition
[
20
]
.

Definition 2.7

(n-local discriminability)
.

Let
n\leq m
. We say that that an OPT satisfies
n-local discriminability
if the effects obtained as a conic combination of the parallel composition of effects
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
,
where
a_{j}
is
k_{j}
-partite with
k_{j}\leq n
for all values of j, are separating for
m
-partite states.

We recall that a
k
-partite effect is an effect on a system that is obtained as the composition of
k
systems.
The case of
n=1
, that is the case of classical and quantum theory, is simply called
local discriminability
. In the latter case, this property tells us that state tomography can be accomplished by means of local measurements only, and it is equivalent to state that for any pair of
systems
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{B}
with size
D_{\mathrm{A}}
and
D_{\mathrm{B}}
respectively, the composite system
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
has size
D_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}=D_{\mathrm{A}}D_{\mathrm{B}}
. Notice that a
theory that is

n
-locally discriminable is also
n+1
-locally discriminable. Fermionic theory, real quantum theory and the bilocal classical theory considered here are examples of strictly bilocal theories, namely they satisfy definition
2.7
with
n=2
but not for
n=1
. An OPT with local discriminability also necessarily satisfies the following feature.

Definition 2.8
.

An OPT satisfies atomicity of parallel composition of states if, for any pair of systems
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
and for all pair of atomic states

\phi\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
and
\psi\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{B})
, the composition

\phi\boxtimes\psi\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})
is atomic.

This property is clearly satisfied by classical and quantum theory, and fermionic theory as well. The latter is a
trivial example showing that the atomicity of parallel composition of states does not imply local discriminability.
Moreover, examples of theories that violate atomicity of parallel composition of states can be constructed, and we
will discuss one in detail in the present analysis.

We now introduce two properties that an OPT can have, and that are necessary for our discussion of the information
content, whose definition will be recalled in the next subsection. These are strong causality and steering.

Definition 2.9

(Causal theories)
.

An OPT satisfies strong causality if for every test
\{\mathscr{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
and every collection of tests
\{\mathscr{B}_{j}^{i}\}_{j\in\mathsf{Y}}

labelled by
j\in\mathsf{Y}
, the collection of events
\{\mathscr{C}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}
with

\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.22217pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.75pt\raise 5.11108pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt\raise-1.98611pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}_{i,j}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.22217pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.75pt\raise 20.44434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.27777pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 8.48132pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-8.48132pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt\raise-2.66666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{A}_{i}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.88867pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.27777pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 8.71513pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-8.71513pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt\raise-2.69987pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{B}_{j}^{i}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.11084pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.61111pt\raise 5.11108pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{C}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.33301pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\

(7)

is a test of the theory.

In other words, any theory that is strongly causal contains all the conceivable conditioned test, namely those in
which the second test that is performed is chosen according to the outcome of the first one. This is a strong notion
of causality, since one can show that the above statement implies uniqueness of the deterministic
effect
[
5
,
8
]
, that corresponds to a weaker form of causality defined as follows.

Definition 2.10

(Weakly causal theories)
.

An OPT satisfies weak causality if for every system type
\mathrm{A}
there is a unique deterministic effect
e_{\mathrm{A}}
.

In a non deterministic theory (i.e. a theory where at least one event of the trivial system is neither 0 nor 1) strong
causality implies convexity of the set of events of every type—including, of course, sets of states of every system
[
21
,
5
]
. An example of an OPT that is weakly but not strongly causal is given in
[
22
]
. For every system of this theory the set of states is a simplex whose vertices, corresponding to the pure states, are perfectly discriminable, just as in standard classical theory. The fact that the set of states is a simplex for every system implies that the theory is weakly causal (by theorem 1 in
[
23
]
). However, the set of allowed tests between non-trivial systems only includes the composition of preparation and observation tests with permutations of subsystems of composite systems, and does not contain conditional tests, i.e., tests whose events are of the form (
7
). This implies that such a theory is not strongly causal.

We now come to the second property of interest, which is the property of steering.
Steering as we define it is actually a property of both classical and quantum theory.

Property 1

(Steering)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
and
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}\subseteq\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
be a refinement of
\rho
. Then there exist a system
\mathrm{B}
, a state
\Psi\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})
, and an
observation test
\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
such that

\ \ \begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.27777pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 7.07724pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-7.07724pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.55554pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\sigma_{i}\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern ,\qquad\forall i\in\mathsf{X}.

Notice that the state
\Psi
in the steering assumption must be a dilation of
\rho
(see definition
2.5
), as one can easily verify upon
summing over
i\in\mathsf{X}
, and noticing that the coarse-graining of all the effects in an observation test yields a deterministic effect. Moreover, we are not requiring that the same dilation
\Psi
steers all the possible
refinements of
\rho
, differently from what happens in quantum theory. We remark indeed that in quantum theory a
stronger version of steering holds. It states that any ensemble of a given state can be steered by means of a
purification of that state. In more precise terms, given a state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
and a purification

\Phi\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})
of
\rho
, for any decomposition
\sum_{i\in\mathsf{X}}p_{i}\sigma_{i}
of
\rho
there exists an
observation test
\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}\subseteq\mathsf{Eff}(\mathrm{B})
such that

\ p_{i}\ \begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\sigma_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\sigma_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.00002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.75pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 20.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern =\ \begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.41666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Phi}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.61111pt\raise 4.49997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.61111pt\raise-15.99991pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 28.61115pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\Phi}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 18.61113pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.54167pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{B}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.61115pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.62094pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-6.31047pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}b_{i}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{b_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.62094pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{b_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.62094pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{b_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.62094pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{b_{i}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\ .\qquad\forall i\in\mathsf{X}.

However, we do not need such a strong property for our purposes. In the following when referring to steering we
will be referring to the property
1
.

Finally, with the following set of definitions we now make explicit what we mean by classical theory. First of all, we
say that an OPT is
convex
when, for all systems of the theory, the set of states (and more generally of
transformations) is convex. As we remarked above, this is always the case for non deterministic theories with strong
causality.

Definition 2.11

(simplicial theory)
.

A simplicial theory is a finite-dimensional OPT where the extremal states of every system
\mathrm{A}
are the vertices
of a
D_{\mathrm{A}}
-simplex.

Definition 2.12

(Joint perfect discriminability)
.

Let
\mathrm{A}
be a system of the theory. A set of states
\{|\rho_{i})_{\mathrm{A}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is jointly perfectly discriminable if there exists an observation test
\{(a_{i}|_{\mathrm{A}}\}
such that:

(a_{i}|\rho_{j})_{\mathrm{A}}\propto\delta_{ij},\qquad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}.

We now define classical theories only in the restricted context of weakly causal theories. The reason is that the
definition would otherwise involve complications that are unnecessary for our present purposes. Indeed, the core of
our analysis will deal with strongly causal theories, which are also weakly causal.

Definition 2.13

(classical theories)
.

A classical theory is a theory where any set of pairwise non proportional atomic states is jointly perfectly discriminable.

As it is clear, a classical theory in the sense of the above definition is not necessarily convex, despite the pure
states are the extremal points of a simplex. However, when strong causality is assumed, the scenario is restricted to
convex theories, and thus classicality in that case implies that the set of states is a simplex whose extremal points
are perfectly discriminable pure states.

While atomicity of parallel composition of states (definition
2.8
) is always implied by local discriminability, the converse is generally not true, a trivial example being fermionic theory, that is strictly bilocal but state-purity preserving. Nevertheless, a converse holds for n-locally discriminable theories that are also simplicial, as a corollary of theorems 2 and 3 in
[
23
]
: firstly, every simplicial theory satisfying n-local discriminability does not admit the presence of entangled states iff it satisfies atomicity of parallel composition of states (theorem 3
[
23
]
). But it is also true that a simplicial theory is locally discriminable iff there exist no entangled states (theorem 2
[
23
]
). Whereby, a simplicial n-locally discriminable theory enjoys atomicity of parallel composition of states iff it is actually locally discriminable.

In the following we will deal with strongly causal classical theories, where
the set of states of any system is a simplex whose extreme points, except for the null state (i.e. the state that yields zero on all the effects), are pure. Thus, atomicity of parallel composition for states is equivalent to purity of parallel composition of pure states.

2.2
Digitizable theories and the information content

In a general physical theory of information, information plays of course a central role. It is then reasonable to
expect that one has a way to quantify it, and this in turn has to come along with a unit. In classical
and quantum theories, the output of any physical source is digitized in terms of bits and qubits, respectively. In
particular, focusing on the case of classical information theory, any string of length
N
, say
\mathbf{i}\coloneqq i_{1}\dots i_{N}
,
where each
i
is extracted from an alphabet of
d
symbols, can be perfectly encoded on a distinct array of a
suitable number of bits. The bit is indeed the reference system that we usually adopt as a unit for assessing the
amount of information of a given classical source; an analogous role is played by the qubit in quantum information
theory. Moreover, notice that one can actually choose any other system for the digitization of the outputs of a
classical or quantum information source, with the only difference being that the entropy function, which numerically
quantifies the information content, must be rescaled by a suitable multiplicative factor.

These simple observations can be thoroughly made into a requirement that a theory must abide by, thus setting the
stage for a meaningful definition of compression rate in the OPT framework. This is formalized as follows.

Definition 2.14

(Digitizability)
.

We say that an OPT is digitizable if there exists a system
\mathrm{B}
(called
o-bit
) such that for any system
\mathrm{X}
there
exists
k<\infty
and a pair of maps
\mathscr{C}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes k})
and

\mathscr{F}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes k}\to\mathrm{X})
such that
\mathscr{F}\circ\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{X}}
. Moreover, if
\mathrm{B}_{1}
and
\mathrm{B}_{2}
are two such systems, then they are asymptotically equivalent.

As we have already mentioned, finite-dimensional classical and quantum theory are digitizable. In particular, all the systems are asymptotically equivalent in the sense of definition
2.1
, and any system can serve as an o-bit. The OPT considered in
[
22
]
, on the other hand, represents
an example of a theory that is not digitisable. This theory was constructed for a different purpose rather than studying digitizability, but it is not difficult to realize that it does not satisfy the latter property. As we have already mentioned, in this theory all systems are classical, in the sense that for any system the set of states is a simplex, and the vertices corresponding to non-null extremal states are perfectly jointly discriminable. Moreover, for any integer
D
there exists a type of system of size
D
. The set of allowed channels between non-trivial systems is restricted to contain compositions of deterministic states and effects with permutations of subsystems of composite systems. It is then clear that, given a system of type
\mathrm{A}
, for every other system
\mathrm{B}
of different type it is not possible to find an integer
M
and a left-reversible map of type
\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}
. Indeed, such a left-reversible map should have the
\mathrm{A}
system as a subsystem at the output in order to be left-reversible, and given the uniqueness of the decomposition of integers in prime factors, this is impossible. Thus, the theory cannot be digitisable.

An
information source
is characterized by a system
\mathrm{A}
and a state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
. Repeated uses of the
source then generate a
message
that, generalizing the i.i.d. setting, has the form of a factorized state,

\rho^{\boxtimes N}
. Given a pair of maps
\mathscr{E}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}\rightarrow\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M})
and

\mathscr{D}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}\rightarrow\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N})
, that we name
encoding
and
decoding

respectively, we need a figure of merit that establishes the goodness of the
codec
map
\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}
. Our choice comes as a consequence of the two following elementary observations:
(i) the output message to which we have local access may be correlated with an ancilla, and (ii) it could be prepared
in different ways. Since the aim is to preserve all the possible information gathered in the message, we use the
following quantity

\displaystyle D(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\Psi\in D_{\rho^{\boxtimes N}}}

\displaystyle\sup_{\{\Gamma_{i}\}\in\mathcal{R}(\Psi)}\sum_{i}\lVert\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}(\Gamma_{i})-\Gamma_{i}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

In other words, it is an optimization of the average error over all the possible decompositions of any dilation of the message.
We then define an
(N,\epsilon)
-compression scheme
as an encoding-decoding pair
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
such that
D(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E})<\epsilon
, and we denote by
E_{N,M,\epsilon}(\rho)
the set of all the
(N,\epsilon)
-compression schemes. We now have all the ingredients for defining the information content of a source.

Definition 2.15

(Information Content)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
and

M_{N,\varepsilon}(\rho)\coloneqq\min\{M:E_{N,M,\varepsilon}(\rho)\neq\emptyset\}.

(8)

We define the smallest achievable compression ratio for length
N
to
tolerance
\varepsilon
as follows

R_{N,\varepsilon}(\rho)\coloneqq\frac{\min\{M:E_{N,M,\varepsilon}(\rho)\neq\emptyset\}}{N}.

(9)

The information content of the state
\rho
is defined as

I(\rho)\coloneqq\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}R_{N,\varepsilon}(\rho).

(10)

The well-posedness of this definition has been proved in
[
18
]
, as a direct consequence of the digitizability assumption. As it is clear, the definition of
I(\rho)
strongly depends on the
choice of the figure of merit
D(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})
. The one that we have introduced takes into account all possible refinements of any dilation of
\rho^{\boxtimes N}
. Alternatively, one can consider the pure convex refinements of any dilation of
\rho^{\boxtimes N}
and define

\displaystyle D^{\text{pur}}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\Psi\in D_{\rho^{\boxtimes N}}}

\displaystyle\sup_{\{p_{i},\Psi_{i}\}\in\mathcal{P}(\Psi)}\sum_{i}p_{i}\lVert\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}(\Psi_{i})-\Psi_{i}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

When the theory satisfies strong causality (property
2.9
), and if any state is proportional to a determistic one,
D
and
D^{\rm pur}
can be equivalently used to define the information content via the formula (
10
). If a theory also satisfies steering (property
1
), along with strong causality, one can use a figure of merit that is further simplified
[
18
]

\displaystyle D^{\text{dil}}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})\coloneqq\sup_{\Psi\in D_{\rho^{\boxtimes N}}}\lVert\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}(\Psi)-\Psi\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(11)

3
A case study: the information content in Bilocal Classical Theory

Bilocal Classical Theory (hereafter BCT) is a fully-fledged operational probabilistic theory that has been constructed with the aim of showing the independence of two fundamental notions: entanglement and complementarity. As we have already recalled, simplicial theories, in the sense of definition
2.11
, admit of the presence of entangled states iff they do not satisfy the local discriminability principle (see Theorem 2 in
[
23
]
), and this can be explicitly seen in the case of BCT. Here we outline the features of the theory that are relevant for the present work, in particular we recall the structure of state spaces, which are essentially the same of those of classical theory, and more importantly the way in which systems are composed in parallel (Postulate 1 and 2 of
[
23
]
). And the characterization of channels. For a detailed account we refer the reader to
[
19
]
.

From the point of view of the state space, BCT does
not differ from standard classical theory. In BCT, for every integer

D>1
it is assumed that there exists a unique system with size

D
. The set of states of each system is given by a simplex whose
vertices are jointly perfectly discriminable, whence BCT is a convex
classical theory, according to definition

2.13
. In particular, for any system
\mathrm{A}
,
the extremal states are the vertices of the simplex
\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
, and
the non-null extremal states, say

\{|i)_{\mathrm{A}}\}_{i=1}^{D_{\mathrm{A}}}
, are perfectly discriminated by
an observation test
\{(j|_{\mathrm{A}}\}_{j=1}^{D_{\mathrm{A}}}
,
i.e.
(j|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=\delta_{ij}
. Moreover, given that the pure states
correspond to the vertices of a simplex, any other state can be
uniquely decomposed in terms of pure ones. In

[
23
]
(see in particular theorem 1 therein) it
has been shown that simplicial theories are necessarily weakly
causal, whereby BCT satisfies weak causality.

The salient features of BCT are a consequence of the
behaviour of parallel composition of states. By theorem 4 in

[
23
]
, in a simplicial theory the state space

\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})
of a composite system
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
is fully characterised
by: i) a choice for the size
D_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
of the composite system

\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
; ii) an unambiguous labeling for the pure states of

\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
as
|(ij)_{k})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
—for

i\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{B}}\}
, and
k
in
finite sets
I_{ij}
—and a choice of probability distributions

p_{k}^{ij}
such that

|i)_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes|j)_{\mathrm{B}}=\sum_{k\in I_{ij}}p_{k}^{ij}|(ij)_{k})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}.

BCT is an explixit example of how the situation described above can
be consistently realised. In the first place, the following rule for
the size of bipartite systems is postulated: for any two systems

\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B}
, the size of the composite system
\mathrm{AB}
is given by:

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}=D_{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A}}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &2D_{\mathrm{A}}D_{\mathrm{B}},&\mbox{if}\ \mathrm{A}\neq\mathrm{I}\neq\mathrm{B},\\
&D_{\mathrm{A}},&\mbox{if}\ \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{I}.\end{aligned}\right.

(12)

Recalling that a theory satisfies local discriminability if and only
if
D_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}=D_{\mathrm{A}}D_{\mathrm{B}}
, the fact that BCT does not enjoy such
a property follows from the above compositional law
(
12
). Actually BCT is strictly
bilocal
[
19
]
, since it satisfies the following
constraint on the dimension of tripartite systems (see theorem 2 in

[
19
]
)

D_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}}=D_{\mathrm{A}}D_{\mathrm{B}}D_{\mathrm{C}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}D_{\mathrm{C}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}}D_{\mathrm{A}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{C}}D_{\mathrm{B}}

where
\Delta_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}\coloneqq D_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}-D_{\mathrm{A}}D_{\mathrm{B}}
, as one can easily
check using equation (
12
).
The unambiguous labeling for the pure states of the composite system is made as follows:
for any
i\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{B}}\}
, the set

I_{ij}
is given by
\{+,-\}
, and
p^{ij}_{k}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}
.
Let
\mathrm{I}\neq\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}
. The set of pure states of any composite system
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
is then

\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})=\{|(ij)_{-})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}},|(ij)_{+})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}\left.|\right.1\leq i\leq D_{\mathrm{A}},1\leq j\leq D_{\mathrm{B}}\}
,
so that for all pure states
|i)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}),|j)_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{B})
the following parallel
composition rule holds:

(13)

When a third system is added the association satisfies the following law:

\displaystyle((ij)_{s_{1}}k)_{s_{2}}=(i(jk)_{s_{1}s_{2}})_{s_{1}},

(14)

for all local indices
i,j,k
and signs
s_{1},s_{2}
.
As already mentioned, as a consequence of bilocality BCT admits of entangled states. More precisely, all the pure states of any bipartite system are entangled
[
19
]
.

According to equation (
13
), each time we compose in parallel a state with itself, there is an additional degree of freedom
s
associated with a sign
+
or
-
which can be either of the two values with the same probability
\frac{1}{2}
. This entails that a message of length
N
that is emitted from a source, described by
\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
, can be written as follows

|\rho^{\boxtimes N})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}}=\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}}}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}|\mathbf{i}_{\mathsf{\bf{s}}})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}},

(15)

where
\mathbf{i}
and
\mathsf{\bf{s}}
collectively denote the string of
N
local indices and of
N-1
signs respectively. Notice that,
according to the rule of Eq. (
14
), the string of signs
\mathsf{\bf{s}}
depends on the order in which the
N
systems
are associated. If the order of composition changes, however, one just has a change in the string of signs

\mathsf{\bf{s}}^{\prime}=f(\mathsf{\bf{s}})
, according to Eq. (
14
), which is immaterial since
\mathsf{\bf{s}}
is a dummy index and
f
is an
invertible function. Anyway, for the sake of clarity, we will ubiquitously adopt the convention that the expression
in Eq. (
15
) refers to the composite system
(\ldots((\mathrm{A}_{1}\mathrm{A}_{2})\mathrm{A}_{3})\ldots\mathrm{A}_{N-1})\mathrm{A}_{N}
.

For our purpose it is of fundamental importance to know how transformations, and in particular channels,
are characterized, since this establishes how much freedom we have in
devising suitable compression schemes
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
. In

[
19
]
the authors first introduce the reversible
transformations as follows (see in particular postulate 3): for any
pair of non-trivial systems
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{A}^{\prime}
of the same size

D_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\mathscr{R}
is a reversible transformation if and only if there
exists a permutation of
D_{\mathrm{A}}
elements and a sign
\tau_{i}
for every
i
such
that for any non trivial
\mathrm{E}
and
|(ij)_{s})\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})

one has

(16)

Then, transformations are introduced as those admitting a reversible

[

]
). More precisely,

The above realisation of a BCT transformation is given in an implicit
form via its reversible simulation, whose action is explicitly defined
in equation (
16
). The following result, proved in

[
19
]
(see in particular lemma 3 and 4 in
appendix A), provides an explicit characterisation of transformations
and channels:

Proposition 3.1

(Characterisation of BCT transformations)
.

\mathscr{C}\in\mathsf{Tr}(\mathrm{A}\rightarrow\mathrm{B})
iff for every
i\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}
there exists a set
\{\lambda_{m,\tau}^{(i)}\}_{(m,\tau)\in I^{(i)}}
,
with
I^{(i)}\subseteq\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{B}}\}\times\{+,-\}
,
\lambda_{m,\tau}^{(i)}>0
for every
(m,\tau)\in I^{(i)}
and
\sum_{(m,\tau)\in I^{(i)}}\lambda^{(i)}_{m,\tau}\leq 1
, such that the following holds for all
|(ij)_{s})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{E}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{E})
:

If the transformation is determinsitic, then
\{\lambda_{m,\tau}^{(i)}\}_{(m,\tau)\in I^{(i)}}
is a probability distribution for every
i
.

We can now analyse the behaviour of the information
content in BCT by applying the formalism developed in subsection

2.2
. The first fact that must be checked is
that BCT is a digitizable theory, in the sense of
definition
2.14
. This is a mandatory step that
allows for a meaningful operational definition of information content,
as discussed at the end of the previous section. The proof is given in
appendix
A
(lemma
A.1
). In BCT,
as in classical and quantum theory, any type of system can serve as
o-bit. Here we choose the type of system with
D=2
, that we will call

bibit
from now on.

The major obstacle in computing the information content of a given state is the complexity of the figure of merit. The
greater is the set of states on which we must validate the compression scheme, the more difficult is to devise one
that works as we wish. However, for any state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
of BCT, one can find a dilation
\Pi\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{E})

from which we can compute all the other ones by applying a suitable channel on the ancillary system
\mathrm{E}
, as it is
stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
and let
\sum_{ijs}q_{ijs}|(ij)_{s})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{F}}=|\Psi)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{F}}\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{F})
be a dilation of
\rho
. Let
\mathrm{E}\cong\mathrm{A}
and
\sum_{iks}p_{iks}|(ik)_{s})=|\Pi)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{E}}\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{E})
be the dilation of
\rho
with joint probability distribution defined as

p_{iks}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\delta_{ik}p_{i},&s=+;\\
&0,&s=-.\end{aligned}\right.

Then, there exists a channel
\mathscr{C}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{E},\mathrm{F})
such that

5.11108pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{E}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern
