3.06668pt\raise-3.41666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.52759pt\raise-11.75539pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.2639pt\raise 5.11108pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{F}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.74976pt\raise-11.75539pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}.

The proof of this proposition and of all the other results of this and the subsequent section are given in appendix
A
.
The above statement, along with the fact that BCT satisfies the strong causality principle and the steering property, straightforwardly implies the following
proposition, which drastically simplifies the task of devising a compression scheme.

Proposition 3.3
.

Given a state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
and a compression scheme
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
for a message of length
N
, the figure of merit can be computed according to the following formula

\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})=\\
\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\bf{i}}\lVert(\mathscr{C}-\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}}^{N})\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{A}^{N}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(17)

In other words, defining
\tilde{I}(\rho)
analogously to
I(\rho)
(equation (
10
)) by replacing
D(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})
with

\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})
, it holds that
I(\rho)=\tilde{I}(\rho)
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathrm{A}
be a BCT system and
\sum_{i=1}^{D_{\mathrm{A}}}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
. Then

I(\rho)=\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}.

(18)

A first corollary of formula (
18
) is that the information content is additive in BCT. Indeed, if
\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
and

\sum_{j}q_{j}|j)_{\mathrm{B}}=|\sigma)_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{B})
then the factorized state is given by

|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes|\sigma)_{\mathrm{B}}=\sum_{i,j,s}\frac{1}{2}p_{i}q_{j}|(ij)_{s})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}},

a straightforward application of equation (
18
) then gives

\displaystyle I(\rho\boxtimes\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+H(\mathsf{\bf{q}})+1]+1}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}+\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{q}})+1}{2}

\displaystyle=I(\rho)+I(\sigma).

We then deduce that atomicity of parallel composition of states is not a necessary condition for the additivity of information content when factorized states are considered. In particular, not even local discriminability is a necessary condition for additivity of
I(\rho)
. The latter fact, however, was already known from

[
24
]
, where it is proven that the information content of a fermionic source is given by the von Neumann entropy of the state representing the source, just as in the case of quantum theory, and fermionic theory is a bi-local theory (see
[
25
,
26
]
).

Another interesting feature of the information content in this theory is that it is strictly positive for all states
of any system. In particular, the Shannon entropy of any sharp probability distribution is vanishing, whence, for any
system
\mathrm{A}
, it holds that

I(\phi)=\frac{1}{2},\qquad\forall\phi\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}).

(19)

This is in contrast with what we know from classical, quantum and fermionic theory, where the information content is vanishing if and only if the state
is pure. In this respect, one is led to give the notion of purity an operational meaning by saying that we have maximal knowledge about a physical system
whenever it is in a pure state. In
[
18
]
(proposition V.2) It has been shown that atomicity of parallel composition of states (definition
2.8
) and the uniqueness of purifications up to reversible transofrmations on the ancillary system, are sufficient conditions for this interpretation. Notably, a converse is also true
[
18
]
(proposition V.2), namely that if
I(\phi)=0
whenever the state
\phi
is pure then state purity is preserved under parallel
composition. Here we explicitly see that in a theory that violates atomicity of parallel composition of states also pure states can have non vanishing information content.

In reference
[
16
]
, the authors address the problem of noiseless coding within the generalised probabilistic theories framework. Under certain assumptions, they prove (see in particular theorem VIII.I in
[
16
]
) that the measurement entropy
S_{1}(\rho)
is a lower bound for the rate of the compression task they define in section VIII. At first glance, this result seems to be in contradiction with theorem
3.1
. However,
we remark that two assumptions are made in order to prove such a result, that are not satisfied by the BCT. The first one is that the composition of fine-grained measurements yields a fine-grained measurement, where the latter is defined in
[
16
]
as a measurement such that all its effects are atomic. BCT does not satisfy this property, because of the violation of the atomicity of parallel composition of states, which also entails that the composition of two fine-grained effects is no longer fine-grained. The second assumption is that, if the dimension of the state space of system
\mathrm{A}
is
d
, then the dimension of the state space of the composite system
\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}
is
d^{N}
(here, with the word
dimension
, we are referring to the notion introduced in
[
16
]
, that is the maximum number of elements of a fine-grained observaiton test). In the case of BCT, for a system
\mathrm{A}
with size
D_{\mathrm{A}}
(recall that the
size
is the dimension of the linear space
\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{A})
), the dimension is
d_{\mathrm{A}}=D_{\mathrm{A}}
, so that for
\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}
we find
d_{\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}}=2^{N-1}D_{\mathrm{A}}^{N}>D_{\mathrm{A}}^{N}
for
N>1
, whence also this assumption is violated in our case. Therefore, our result is not in contradiction with theorem VIII.I presented in reference
[
16
]
, since BCT is outside the domain of validity of that theorem. Nevertheless, this result has its relevance for theories in which parallel composition preserves atomicity, in particular in the case of PR-boxes, which represents a very interesting case to investigate.

4
Comparing the information content with entropies

Entropic-like quantities have been introduced in the context of Generalized Probabilistic Theories in terms of the Shannon entropy function
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
.
We report just below their definitions for the sake of completeness.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
for some system
\mathrm{A}
, denote by
\mathcal{O}^{\rm at}(\mathrm{A})
the set of atomic observation tests of

\mathrm{A}
(i.e. observation tests whose elements are atomic effects).
We then define

\displaystyle S_{1}(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\{a_{j}\}\in\mathcal{O}^{\rm at}(\mathrm{A})}H(J)

(20)

\displaystyle S_{2}(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{(p_{i},\{\phi_{i}\})\in\mathcal{P}(\rho)}\sup_{\{a_{j}\}\in\mathcal{O}^{\rm at}(\mathrm{A})}H(I:J)

(21)

\displaystyle S_{3}(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{(p_{i},\{\phi_{i}\})\in\mathcal{P}(\rho)}H(I)

(22)

where in the Shannon entropies and the mutual information on the r.h.s. of the above equations refer to the random variables
I,J
distributed
according to the joint probability distribution
q(i,j)\coloneqq p_{i}(a_{j}|\phi_{i})_{\mathrm{A}}
.
For any
i=1,2,3
we introduce the regularized version of
S_{i}
as follows:

S^{\rm reg}_{i}(\rho)\coloneqq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{S_{i}(\rho^{\boxtimes^{N}})}{N}.

Elementary properties such as concavity, strong concavity and subadditivity have been studied for these quantities; moreover, they have been proven to be not equivalent. As we have seen in the foregoing section, the relation between the information content of a state and the Shannon entropy of the associated probability distribution is not trivial, as a consequence of the violation of atomicity of parallel composition of states. The latter property has also a remarkable consequence on the
behaviour of the regularized entropies with respect to their single-system counterparts, which is stated in the proposition below.

Proposition 4.1
.

In any classical theory, for any system
\mathrm{A}
and
\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{A})
, one has
S_{i}(\rho)=H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
. Moreover, whenever atomicity of parallel composition of states (definition
2.8
) is violated, there exists a state
\Sigma\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{C})
for some system
\mathrm{C}

such that the strict inequality
S_{i}^{\rm reg}(\Sigma)>S_{i}(\Sigma)
holds for any
i=1,2,3
.

As a corollary of the above proposition, we can also notice that, for classical theories violating
property
2.8
, each entropy, in addition to being superadditive, also violates additivity
when factorized states are considered. Indeed, if there exist
|i)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A})
and

|j)_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{B})
such that
|i)\boxtimes|j)
is mixed, we immediately see that

S_{i}(|i)\boxtimes|j))>0=S_{i}(|i))+S_{i}(|j)).

where
S_{i}
is given by the Shannon entropy of the respective decompositions, according to proposition
4.1
.

While in classical and quantum theory all the
S_{i}
’s and their regularized version collapse to the Shannon and von
Neumann entropies, respectively, thus boiling down to the same operational interpretation given by the noiseless
coding theorems, much less is known about their operational meaning in a general theory. In BCT the regularized
entropies are related to the Shannon entropy of the state according to the following proposition.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}=|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
, then
S_{i}^{\rm reg}(\rho)=S_{i}(\rho)+1=H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1
.

A remarkable corollary of this proposition is that, in general, none of the
S_{i}
’s nor the
S_{i}^{\rm reg}
’s can be understood as the minimal compression rate.
Notice that, while the additivity property for factorized states is violated by all the entropies, it is satisfied by the regularized versions. The result of proposition

4.2
is by far intuitive if we think of the particular compositional rule on states that BCT satisfies. Indeed, at the level of single systems, there is no difference
with respect to standard classical theory, and this is true for any classical theory that does not satisfy atomicity of parallel composition of states. The effect of this violation shows up when we consider
N
copies of the same state, the latter operation giving an extra flat bit, one for each additional copy of the original state, and the appearence of this extra bit is captured by the regularized entropies. The factor 2 in the information content can also be intuitively expected, since also when we compose bibits we get additional space that can be used to allocate the message. Therefore, the departure of
I(\rho)
from
S^{\rm reg}_{i}(\rho)
can be essentialy ascribed to the weird compositional rule for systems.

We notice that the results of theorem
3.1
and proposition
4.2
are consistent with the following general bound
from
[
18
]
(in particular, see the proof of lemma V.2 and the result of lemma V.1),

I(\rho)\geq\frac{S_{2}(\rho)}{\log_{2}D},

where
D
is a costant such that
D_{\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}}\leq kD^{M}
for some
k
. Indeed, in the case of BCT
D_{\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}}=\frac{1}{2}4^{M}
, whence

D=4
. Actually, in the present case such a bound is saturated with
S_{2}(\rho)
replaced by
S_{2}^{\rm reg}(\rho)
.

5
Conclusion

We have presented a full computation of the information content in a bilocal classical theory. The result is given in
terms of the Shannon entropy of the probability distribution defining the state, namely, if

|\rho)=\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)
, it turns out that
I(\rho)=(H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1)/{2}
. The definition of
I(\rho)
is
hardly amenable to direct calculation in general theories. However, in the special case of BCT, the calculation is simplified by the fact that, for any state, there exists a
“mother” dilation from which we can obtain all the other ones by applying a suitable channel on the ancilla. With
respect to standard classical theory, the information content shows two differences that can be both ascribed to the
violation of atomicity of parallel composition of states in BCT: i) there is an overhead given by +1 in the numerator.
This is due to the appearance of a bit each time that we compose in parallel a new copy of the same state. Since each
bit is uniformly distributed we end up with the maximum overhead, that is indeed +1; ii) there is a factor 2 in the
denominator. This follows from the fact that, when we compose bibits into registers, their dimension is given by
the formula
D_{\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}}=2^{M-1}D_{\mathrm{B}}^{M}
, thus the room for allocating messages per single bibit is almost “double” with respect to
the size of the register. Notice that the factor 2 in the denominator is
then related to meaning of information content in the specific theory at hand, where the elementary systems for physical encoding are bibits. If we had to evaluate the
classical
information content, i.e. the ability of the source to encode classical information, then the regularized mutual information would be the right quantifier.

We have already noticed that the information content in BCT is additive on factorized states—i.e. states of
the form
\rho\boxtimes\sigma
—and this means that atomicity of parallel composition is not a necessary condition
for additivity. A question that remains open is under which hypotheses, given two states

\rho,\sigma\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
, one is able to prove that
I(\rho\boxtimes\sigma)=I(\rho)+I(\sigma)
. It might be also the
case that additivity is a feature of
I(\rho)
that holds in full generality, as it would be desirable for a measure of the information content. The results of the present work seem to suggest
that atomicity of parallel composition plays a marginal role for this property.

Along with the information content we have also analyzed the behaviour of three different entropic functions that have
been considered in the literature. At the level of single system there is no difference with respect to standard
classical theory, and they all coincide with the Shannon entropy of the state
\rho
. As a consequence, any classical
theory is monoentropic
[
15
]
. The regularized entropies are clearly sensible to the extra bit that
arises when systems are composed, and they all turn out to be equal to
H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1
. This result then establishes the
existence of theories of information where none of the proposed generalizations of entropy can be interpreted as the
information content of the source, and neither their regularized versions do. Remarkably, this is true in a thory that
is monoentropic. The departure of the regularized version from the single-system counterpart is not peculiar of the
BCT, but it actually takes place in any classical theory (in the sense of the definition
2.13
)
whenever atomicity of parallel composition of states is violated, as proved in
proposition
4.1
.

As we have already noticed at the end of the last section, in the case of BCT there is a relation between the
regularized entropy
S_{2}^{\rm reg}(\rho)
and the information content of the following form

I(\rho)=\frac{S_{2}^{\rm reg}(\rho)}{\log_{2}D},

(23)

where
D
is a constant such that the relation
D_{\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}}\leq kD^{M}
holds for some
k
. In the case of BCT
\mathrm{B}
is the bibit, and this relation is saturated with
k=\frac{1}{2}
and
D=4
, whence the equation above trivially holds. One might be tempted to conjecture that a result of this form holds for any classical
theory, but it is not difficult to realize that this is not the case. Let us consider a classical theory
with only one type of system, say the bit (whose size is 2), satisfying local
discriminability and, consequently, atomicity of parallel composition. Now, restrict the allowed tests
of the theory to be preparation tests, observation tests, and all possible permutations of the bits when more bits are composed in parallel. It is easy to realize that there are
no protocols that allow one to compress a source represented by a mixed state of a single bit, so that
I(\rho)=1
. But proposition
4.1

implies that
S_{i}(\rho)=h(p)
where
h(p)
is the binary entropy of the bit state
\rho
, and so is for
S_{i}^{\rm reg}(\rho)
by local discriminability,
whence the conjecture is false.

The main lesson that we learn from the results presented so far is that a treatment of the notion of information
content, from a foundational point of view, cannot ignore the compositional structure of a physical theory, as we have
seen that the latter heavily marks its behaviour also in a classical theory. Moreover, as we have just argued, also
the allowed transformations play a significant role, as they might severely
restrict the freedom of compressing.

We leave open for further studies what happens if one considers non-local classical theories with a no-restriction
hypothesis on the allowed transformations. It might be the case that a relation very similar in form to
equation (
23
) holds.
Aiming at doing a first step beyond the the non-local classical scenario, one could ask how the present formalism can be applied to other known non-local theories, in order to investigate possible deviations of the information content from the expected behaviour in the absence of local discriminability. Recently, the authors studied information compression in fermionic theory
[
24
]
, which is a strictly bilocal theory, showing that the information content equals the von Neumann entropy of the fermionic state, and a similar analysis could be carried out for real quantum theory, which also violates the local discriminability. Another model that could highlight relevant features of the information content is that of Ref.
[
27
]
, where finite-dimensional real, complex and quaternionic quantum theory have been unified within a single category, where complex quantum systems compose in a non-trivial way.
Another interesting question is what happens in the case of the Popescu-Rohrlich
boxes
[
28
]
. It is possible that one of the entropies is equal to the information content. On the one hand, we
already know that
I(\rho)\geq S_{2}(\rho)
, therefore the missing part is achievability, namely the direct part of a
noiseless coding theorem, which would establish
I(\rho)=S_{2}(\rho)
. The question in the case of PR-boxes is particularly relevant in light of the fact that the three entropies are known to be inequivalent in such a context.

Answering fundamental questions about information content and entropic functions is a first step toward the formulation of area laws and holographic principle beyond the standard quantum scenario. Both area laws and the holographic principle rely on the notion of entropy, which in turn is operationally interpreted as a quantifier of uncertainty (or information content). Understanding to what extent those laws can be generalized, independently of the nature of the systems involved, may shed new light on the relation between microscopic and large-scale physical phenomena in terms of localization and flow of information
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
.

P.P. and L.V. acknowledge financial support from European Union - Next Generation EU through the PNRR MUR Project No. PE0000023. A.T. acknowledges the financial support of Elvia and Federico Faggin Foundation (Silicon Valley Community Foundation Project ID No. 2020- 214365).

Appendix A
Proofs of the main results

Lemma A.1
.

BCT is a digitizable theory.

Proof.

We show that any system
\mathrm{B}
of any size
D_{\mathrm{B}}
can serve as o-bit for BCT. Indeed, let
\mathrm{A}
be any other system of the theory, denote by
D_{\mathrm{A}}
its size and set

k=\left\lceil\log_{2D_{\mathrm{B}}}2D_{\mathrm{A}}\right\rceil.

(24)

Then, let
\mathsf{\bf{h}}:\{1,\cdots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}\rightarrow\{1,\cdots,D_{\mathrm{B}}\}^{k}\times\{+,-\}^{k-1}
be an injiective function.
To denote the pair
({\bf j,\bf s})\in\{1,\cdots,D_{\mathrm{B}}\}^{k}\times\{+,-\}^{k-1}
we use the shortened notation
\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}}
.
The action of the encoding

\mathscr{E}
and the decoding
\mathscr{D}
are defined by two set of probability distributions,
\lambda^{(i)}_{\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}},\tau}
and
\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}})}_{i,\tau}
respectively. We
then set
\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{h}}(i),+}^{(i)}=1

\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}
for the encoding. For the decoding,
\forall\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}}
such that
\exists!i
satisfying
\mathsf{\bf{h}}(i)=\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}}
we define
\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}})}_{i,+}=1
, while for every other
\mathsf{\bf{j_{s}}}
we can freely choose any probability distribution at our wish. It is now easy to realize that, for any
i,j
and
\mathrm{E}
, the following holds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\ .

(25)

Now consider two systems of BCT, say
\mathrm{B}_{1}
and
\mathrm{B}_{2}
. For any integer number
k_{1}
of systems
\mathrm{B}_{1}
, the minimal number
M^{\rm min}_{2}(k_{1})
of
\mathrm{B}_{2}
that are needed to perform the encoding in a perfectly recoverable way as in equation (
25
) (with
\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{B}_{1}^{\boxtimes k_{1}}
) is given by formula (
24
)

M_{2}^{\rm min}(k_{1})=\left\lceil k_{1}\log_{2D_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}}2D_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\right\rceil,

and similarly

M_{1}^{\rm min}(k_{2})=\left\lceil k_{2}\log_{2D_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}}2D_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\right\rceil.

Therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{k_{1}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M_{2}^{\rm min}(k_{1})}{k_{1}}

\displaystyle=\log_{2D_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}}2D_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\lim_{k_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M_{1}^{\rm min}(k_{2})}{k_{2}}\right)^{-1},

as required.
∎

Since the set of states is a simplex, the pure ones are affinely independent, and this entails that the condition

\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}|\Pi)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{E}}=|\Psi_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{F}})
is satisfied iff the following equation holds for any
i,j
and
s

q_{ijs}=\sum_{\tau,k}p_{ik\tau}\lambda_{j(s\tau)}^{(k)}.

(26)

For
p_{ik\tau}
as in the statement

q_{ijs}=\sum_{\tau,k}p_{ik\tau}\lambda_{j(s\tau)}^{(k)}=\sum_{k}\delta_{ik}p_{i}\lambda_{js}^{(k)}=p_{i}\lambda_{js}^{(i)}.

(27)

Since
\sum_{j,s}q_{ijs}=p_{i}
, for those indices
i
such that
p_{i}=0
the equation trivially holds whatever the choice of
\lambda^{(i)}_{js}
is, while for those
i
’s such that
p_{i}>0
,
\lambda_{js}^{(i)}=\frac{q_{ijs}}{p_{i}}
is an admissible solution.
∎

Proof of proposition
3.3
.

Given any state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}_{1}(\mathrm{A})
, define the following dilation of
\rho^{\boxtimes N}
(see equation (
15
)) .

|\Pi^{N})\coloneqq\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i}},\mathsf{\bf{s}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{A}^{N}}.

Moreover, for the sake of clarity let us set

\displaystyle E_{N,M,\varepsilon}^{\rm pur}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\{(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})|D^{\rm pur}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})<\varepsilon\},

\displaystyle E_{N,M,\varepsilon}^{\rm dil}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\{(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})|D^{\rm dil}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})<\varepsilon\},

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{N,M,\varepsilon}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\{(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})|\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})<\varepsilon\}.

Denote by
I^{\rm pur}(\rho)
,
I^{\rm dil}(\rho)
,
\tilde{I}(\rho)
the corresponding quantities defined in a way analogous to (
10
).
The purpose is to show that
I(\rho)=\tilde{I}(\rho)
. We already know that
I(\rho)=I^{\rm pur}(\rho)=I^{\rm dil}(\rho)
. Since it is clear that

\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})\leq D^{\rm pur}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})
, it follows that
E^{\rm pur}_{N,M,\varepsilon}(\rho)\subseteq\tilde{E}_{N,M\varepsilon}(\rho)
and thus
\tilde{I}(\rho)\leq I^{\rm pur}(\rho)=I(\rho)
. On the other hand,
by proposition
3.2
, lemma
2.1
(monotonicity of the operational norm with respect to channels), and the triangle inequality one also has, for any
\Psi\in D_{\rho^{\boxtimes N}}
and
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})\in\tilde{E}_{N,M,\varepsilon}(\rho)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{E}}|\Psi)_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{E}}-|\Psi)_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{E}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lVert\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{E}}|\Pi^{N})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{E}}-|\Pi^{N})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{E}})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{E}}|\Pi^{N})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{E}}-|\Pi^{N})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{E}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})

whence
D^{\rm dil}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})\leq\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})
. This implies the inequality
I^{\rm dil}(\rho)\leq\tilde{I}(\rho)
. We have then proved that
I(\rho)=\tilde{I}(\rho)
.
∎

In order to relate the information content
I(\rho)
to the Shannon entropy
H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
of the probability distribution defining the unique pure state decomposition of
\rho
, we use standard techniques from classical information theory. In particolar we remind the reader of the notion of typical
strings and of typical set, along with some properties of the latter.

Let
\mathsf{\bf{p}}=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
be a probability distribution with
\mathsf{X}
a finite outcome set whose label are denoted by
i
. For a given
N
, consider the string
{\bf i}\coloneqq i_{1}\dots i_{N}
associated with the factorized probability
p_{\mathbf{i}}\coloneqq p_{i_{1}}\cdots p_{i_{N}}
. For any
\delta>0
, the (
N,\delta
)
-typical set

T^{N}_{\delta}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
is a subset of all the possible strings of lentgh
N
and it is defined as follows

T^{N}_{\delta}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})=\left\{{\bf i}:\left|\frac{1}{N}\log_{2}\frac{1}{p_{\bf i}}-H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})\right|\leq\delta\right\},

where
H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})\coloneqq-\sum_{i\in\mathsf{X}}p_{i}\log_{2}p_{i}
denotes the Shannon entropy of the probability distribution
\mathsf{\bf{p}}
. Correspondingly, a string belonging to
T^{N}_{\delta}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
is called a
typical string
. Notice that by reformulating the definition of typical set, a string is typical iff the associated probability is bounded as follows

2^{-N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+\delta]}\leq p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\leq 2^{-N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})-\delta]}

(28)

The typical set has the following relevant properties, that will be extensively used in the proof of theorem
3.1

Theorem A.1
.

Let
\mathsf{\bf{p}}
be a probability distribution, and for
\delta>0
let
T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
denote the (
N,\delta
)-typical set. Then

1.

Let
\eta>0
. Then there exists
N_{0}
such that for any
N\geq N_{0}

P({\bf i}\in T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}}))\geq 1-\eta;

2.

Let
\eta>0
. Then there exists
N_{0}
such that, for any
N\geq N_{0}
, the cardinality of
T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
is bounded as follows

(1-\eta)2^{N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})-\delta]}\leq|T^{N}_{\delta}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})|\leq 2^{N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+\delta]}.

This is a standard result, whose proof can be found in any of the following references
[
32
,
33
]
. With this tool in our hand, we can prove the following

Lemma A.2
.

Let
S(N)
be any collection of strings
(\mathsf{\bf{i}},\mathsf{\bf{s}})
, with
\mathsf{\bf{i}}\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}^{N}
and
\mathsf{\bf{s}}\in\{+,-\}^{N-1}
, such that
|S(N)|=2^{2NR-1}
with
R<\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}
fixed. Then, for any
\eta>0
there exists
N_{0}
such that
for any
N\geq N_{0}
one has

\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}})\in S(N)}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}<\eta

Proof.

Let
S
be the set of all the possible strings
\mathsf{\bf{s}}
,
\Delta\coloneqq H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1-2R>0
and define

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(T_{\frac{\Delta}{2}}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})\times S)\cap S(N),

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(T_{\frac{\Delta}{2}}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})^{c}\times S)\cap S(N),

where
A^{c}
denotes the complementary set of
A
. Then consider

\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}})\in S(N)}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}=\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}})\in S_{1}}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}+\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}})\in S_{2}}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}.

The first term is bounded as follows thanks to the definition of typical set (see equation (
28
))

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}})\in S_{1}}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})-\frac{\Delta}{2}]-N+1}|S(N)|

\displaystyle=2^{-N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})-\frac{\Delta}{2}]-N+1+2NR-1}

\displaystyle=2^{-N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1-2R-\frac{\Delta}{2}]}<\eta/2

provided that we take
N\geq N_{1}
with
N_{1}
sufficiently large. For the term with
S_{2}
we use item 1 of theorem
A.1
, which implies that for
\eta/2>0
there exists
N_{2}
such that for any
N\geq N_{2}
we have
\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in T_{\frac{\Delta}{2}}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})^{c}}p_{\mathbf{i}}<\frac{\eta}{2}
. We then have

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathsf{\bf{s}})\in S_{2}}p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}\leq\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in T_{\frac{\Delta}{2}}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})^{c}}p_{\mathbf{i}}<\frac{\eta}{2}.

Setting
N_{0}=\max\{N_{1},N_{2}\}
we have the thesis.
∎

Before giving the proof we provide an explicit formula to compute the figure of merit in equation (
29
) in terms
of the probability distributions that characterise the encoding and decoding channels. Let us consider a BCT source represented by a
state
|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{X}}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}
on a system
\mathrm{A}
, where
\{p_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{X}}
is a probability distribution. Fix the number
N
of copies of
\mathrm{A}
and consider a compression scheme
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
for
M
bibits, i.e.
\mathscr{E}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}\to\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M})
and
\mathscr{D}\in\mathsf{Tr}_{1}(\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}\to\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N})
. By proposition
3.1
,
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{D}
are characterised by two probability distributions,
\{\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\}
and
\{\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}\}
respectively, where
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
,
\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}
is a shortened notation for the pairs
(\mathsf{\bf{i}},{\bf s})\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}^{N}\times\{+,-\}^{N-1}
. and
({\bf t},{\bf n})\in\{1,2\}^{M}\times\{+,-\}^{M-1}
respectively. Now, recall that the figure of merit is computed according to proposition
3.3

\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E})=\\
\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\bf{i}}\lVert(\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}-\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{A}^{N}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.

(29)

For any
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
consider the norm

\displaystyle\lVert

\displaystyle(\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}-\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{A}^{N}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\lVert\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},{\bf i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}},\tau,\tau^{\prime}}\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{{\bf i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}\tau^{\prime}}|({\bf i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})-|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\lVert\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},{\bf i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}\neq\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}},\tau,\tau^{\prime}}\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{{\bf i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}\tau^{\prime}}|({\bf i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},\tau\neq\tau^{\prime}}\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau^{\prime}}|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{-})

\displaystyle\left(-1+\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},\tau}\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}},\tau}\right)|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

where we have decomposed the sum into three terms.
For any element
\rho
in

\mathsf{St}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{A})
, say
\rho=\sum_{i}c_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}
, we have
\lVert\rho\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=\sum_{i}|c_{i}|
. Thereofre, using the normalisation conditions
for
\{\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\}
and
\{\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}\}

\displaystyle\lVert

\displaystyle(\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E}-\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})\boxtimes\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{A}^{N}})|(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{A}^{N}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=2\left(1-\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},\tau}\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}},\tau}\right)

and the figure of merit then becomes

\tilde{D}(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E})=2\left(1-\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},\tau}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}\right).

(30)

Proof of theorem
3.1
.

We first prove the achievability, namely that
I(\rho)\leq\frac{H(\{p_{i}\})+1}{2}
. Let
\delta>0
, and for any
N
consider the following number of bibits

M=\left\lceil N\left[\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}+\delta\right]\right\rceil

by item
2
of theorem
A.1
and the above choice of
M
we have that,
\forall N\in\mathbb{N}

|T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})|2^{N-1}\leq 2^{N[H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1+\delta]-1}\leq 2^{2M-1}=D_{\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M}}.

This bound entails the existence of a subset
\mathcal{P}_{N}\subseteq\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{B}^{\boxtimes M})
with cardinality equal to
|T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})|2^{N-1}
. Denote by
S_{\mathcal{P}_{N}}
the set of strings associated with
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. We will use the same notation that we introduced in the proof of lemma
A.1
and below the proof of lemma
A.2
: any element in
S_{\mathcal{P}_{N}}
is specified by
M
signs
{\bf t}=t_{1},\dots,t_{M}
, one for each
\mathrm{B}
in the composition, and
M-1
additional signs
{\bf n}=n_{1},\dots,n_{M-1}
emerging from the violation of purity of parallel composition. We then denote any element of
S_{\mathcal{P}_{N}}
with the multi-index
\bf t_{n}
. We now define the following
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
compression scheme for any
N
:

1.

The encoding
\mathscr{E}
is defined by a set of probability distributions
\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}
, one for each multi-index
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
, where
{\bf i}\in\{1,\dots,D_{\mathrm{A}}\}^{N}
and
{\bf s}\in\{+,-\}^{N-1}
(recall the notation in the proof of lemma
A.1
). Let
h:T^{N}_{\delta}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})\times\{+,-\}^{N-1}\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{P}_{N}}
be any injective function that associates each typical string
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
with a distinct element
\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}
of
S_{\mathcal{P}_{N}}
. Then, for any such
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
we set
\lambda_{h(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})+}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}=1
. In particular, for any
\mathsf{\bf{i}}\in T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
and any
\mathsf{\bf{s}}\in\{+,-\}^{N-1}
we have the following diagrammatic equation

.

If
\mathsf{\bf{i}}\not\in T^{N}_{\delta}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
, for any

for a fixed
{\bf\bar{t}_{\bar{s}}}\in S_{\mathcal{P}_{N}}
.

is represented as follows

Notice that, having defined
\lambda^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}
for any
\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}
and
\tau
and for any
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
, we have fully specified the action of
\mathscr{E}
on all the pure states of
\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}\boxtimes\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes N}
even if not all of them directly intervene in the evaluation of the figure of merit.

2.

\{\mu_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}\}

\mu_{h^{-1}(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})+}^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}=1

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\ .

Now, with this scheme and using item
1
of theorem
A.1
, we have that for any
\eta>0
there exists
N_{0}
such that for any
N\geq N_{0}
the following holds

\displaystyle D(\rho^{\boxtimes N},\mathscr{C})=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bf{i}\not\in T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}}),\mathsf{\bf{s}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\bf{i}}\lVert|(\bar{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}_{\bar{\mathsf{\bf{s}}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{\mathrm{A}^{N}\mathrm{A}^{N}}-|\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})_{+})_{A^{N}A^{N}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\bf{i}\not\in T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}}),\mathsf{\bf{s}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\bf{i}}\leq 2\sum_{\bf{i}\not\in T_{\delta}^{N}(\mathsf{\bf{p}})}p_{\bf{i}}\leq 2\eta

which in turns implies that, given
\epsilon=2\eta
, for any
N\geq N_{0}
holds true that
E_{N,M,\epsilon}(\rho)\neq\emptyset
. Therefore, for such values of
N
we have
M_{N,\epsilon}(\rho)/N\leq M/N
(recall definition
2.15
), and this implies, by taking the
\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}
and then the
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}

I(\rho)\leq\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}+\delta,

and the thesis follows by the arbitrariness of
\delta
.

Now we prove the minimality of
\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}
, namely that
I(\rho)\geq\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}
. Fix an arbitrary real positive number
\delta>0
and let
\overline{M}
and
\overline{N}
be such that

\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}-\delta\leq\frac{\overline{M}}{\overline{N}}<\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}.

(31)

We then show that there exists
N_{0}
such that for any integer
k
satisfying

k\overline{N}>N_{0}
it holds that
E_{k\overline{N},k\overline{M},\epsilon}(\rho)=\emptyset
for any

\epsilon\in(0,\overline{\epsilon}]
for some
\overline{\epsilon}
.
Let
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
be a compression scheme for messages of length
k\overline{N}
with
k\overline{M}
bibits and let
\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}
,
\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}
be the probability distributions defining the action of
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{D}
respectively on pure states of
\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes k\overline{N}}\mathrm{A}^{\boxtimes k\overline{N}}
. Recall the expression of the figure of merit given in equation (
30
):

D(\rho^{\boxtimes k\overline{N}},\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E})=2\left(1-\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},\tau}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}\right).

(32)

Now consider the sum between brackets. Since all the terms
\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau^{\prime}}
are non-negative, we can upper bound the sum over
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
by a sum over independent indices
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
, obtaining

\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}},\tau}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\tau}\leq\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}

(33)

where
\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\coloneqq\sum_{\tau}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}\tau}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}
and similarly for
\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}
. Now we check that the right hand side of the inequality
written above is bounded as follows (we set
p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}=p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}
)

\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\leq\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}\in\mathsf{I}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}},

(34)

where
\mathsf{I}
is a set with cardinality at most
2^{2k\overline{M}-1}
; indeed, by repeatedly using the inequality
\sum_{k}a_{k}b_{k}\leq\max_{k}\{b_{k}\}
valid for
b_{k}\geq 0
and
\sum_{k}a_{k}=1
we find the following bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\leq\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\mu_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{\bar{t}_{\bar{n}}}}(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}))}

(35)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}:\mathsf{\bf{\bar{t}_{\bar{n}}}}(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})=\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\mu_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{\bar{t}_{\bar{n}}}}(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}))}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}

where, for any
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
,
\mathsf{\bf{\bar{t}_{\bar{n}}}}(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})
is a fixed multi-index that corresponds to the maximum value
\mu_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{\bar{t}_{\bar{n}}}}(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}))}
. In the last sum we have only one
\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}
for any
\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}
, therefore, since the cardinality of the set of strings
\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}
is
2^{2k\overline{M}-1}
, the sum is over at most
2^{2k\overline{M}-1}
terms, as claimed.

Now, notice that our choice of
M_{k}=k\overline{M}
entails

2^{2M_{k}-1}=2^{2\frac{\overline{M}}{\overline{N}}N_{k}-1}
.
Since
\frac{\overline{M}}{\overline{N}}<\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}
(see equation (
31
)), lemma
A.2
can be applied, so that given
\eta>0
there exists
N_{0}
such that for any
k
satisfying
k\overline{N}>N_{0}
we have
\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}<\eta
. By (
33
) and (
35
), we have (recall again (
30
))

\displaystyle D(\rho^{\boxtimes k\overline{N}},\mathscr{D}\mathscr{E})

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(1-\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}\lambda_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}^{(\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}})}\mu^{(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(1-\sum_{\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}}}p_{\mathsf{\bf{i_{s}}}(\mathsf{\bf{t_{n}}})}\right)>2(1-\eta).

And this holds for any compression scheme
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
for
k\overline{N}
usages of the source with
k\overline{M}
bibits. This implies that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,2(1-\eta)]

E_{k\overline{N},k\overline{M},\epsilon}=\emptyset,\qquad\forall k>\left\lceil\frac{N_{0}}{\overline{N}}\right\rceil.

Therefore, for any such
k
, we have the following chain of inequalities

\displaystyle\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}-\delta\leq\frac{k\overline{M}}{k\overline{N}}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M_{k\overline{N},\epsilon}(\rho)}{k\overline{N}}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M_{N,\epsilon}(\rho)}{N}.

The first and the second inequality follow by equation (
31
) and the definition of
M_{k\overline{N},\varepsilon}(\rho)
respectively, while the third inequality follows by the fact that the
\limsup
of a real sequence is always greater than the
\limsup
of any of its subsequence. Taking the
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}
we have
\frac{H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1}{2}-\delta\leq I(\rho)
and the thesis follows by the arbitrariness of
\delta
.
∎

Before giving the proof of proposition
4.1
we recall the following lemma (lemma 1 in
[
23
]
).

Lemma A.3
.

Consider a simplicial OPT satisfying
n
-local discriminability for some positive integer
n
. Then for all systems
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{B}
and non-null extremal states

|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
of the composite system
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
, there exists a unique product of non-null extremal states
|i_{k})_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes|j_{k})_{\mathrm{B}}
such that
|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
convexly refines
|i_{k})_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes|j_{k})_{\mathrm{B}}
.

Since we are focusing on strongly causal theories, pure states are all and only the non-null extremal states, as already mentioned at the end of subsection
2.1
.
The above lemma tells us that for any
|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B})
there exist
|i_{k})_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A})
,
|j_{k})_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{B})
and a probability distribution
\{p_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathsf{L}}
such that

|i_{k})_{\mathrm{A}}\boxtimes|j_{k})_{\mathrm{B}}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathsf{L}}p_{\ell}|\ell)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}

(36)

and
|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}=|\ell)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
for some
\ell\in\mathsf{L}
.
It easy to see that for any pure state
|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
of a bipartite system
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}
, both

(j_{k}|_{\mathrm{B}}|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
and
(i_{k}|_{\mathrm{A}}|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
are also pure.
Indeed, if we now apply the effect
(j_{k}|_{\mathrm{B}}
on
\mathrm{B}
to the equation above, by purity of
|i_{k})
it follows
|i_{k})_{\mathrm{A}}=(j_{k}|_{\mathrm{B}}|\ell)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
for any
\ell\in\mathsf{L}
, and in particular for
|k)
, which is then pure. Similarly if one applies the effect
|i_{k})
on
\mathrm{A}
. It will be useful to keep in mind this fact in the proof of proposition
4.1

Proof of proposition
4.1
.

By hypothesis, there exist systems
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
and
|i)_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathsf{PurSt}(\mathrm{A})
,

such that

where

.

|\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
, that can be decomposed as follows

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern and, while the left hand side is mixed by hypothesis, the right hand side should be pure, since the whole state is assumed to be pure (see the discussion below lemma
A.3
). We then conclude that
|k)_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}\boxtimes|k^{\prime})_{\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}}
must be necessarily mixed. Therefore,
\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2}
has the following decomposition in terms of pure states

where at least one of the conditioned probability distributions
\{q_{\ell|k,k^{\prime}}\}_{\ell}
is non-trivial. Now, notice that for any system
\mathrm{A}
and any state

|\rho)_{\mathrm{A}}=\sum_{i}p_{i}|i)_{\mathrm{A}}
one has
S_{i}(\rho)=H(\{p\})
. The argument that we give here is the same as the one proposed in
[
17
]
for classical theory (see in particular proposition 13 and theorem 3(i)), but since states are separating for effects in any OPT it also applies to the case of
any
classical theory defined according to definition
2.13
. The case of
S_{3}
is pretty obvious, given the uniqueness of the decomposition in terms of pure states. Now, since states are separating for effects, any other atomic effect is proportional to an effect of the perfectly discriminating test, i.e.,
(a|_{\mathrm{A}}=\lambda(i|_{\mathrm{A}}
for some
i
. Thus, for any state
\rho
of a classical theory, it follows that any other atomic observation test
\{a_{i}\}
is such that
H(a_{i}(\rho))\geq H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
, by concavity of the function
x\log x
, and the equality is achieved for the perfectly discriminating test, whence
S_{1}(\rho)=H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
. Finally, notice that for any state
\rho
of a classical theory one has

S_{2}(\rho)=H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})-\inf_{\{a_{i}\}\in\mathcal{O}^{\rm at}}H(X|J)

and
H(X|J)=0
for the perfectly discriminating test.
Now, a trivial computation shows that

\displaystyle S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k,k^{\prime},\ell}p_{k}p_{k^{\prime}}q_{\ell|k,k^{\prime}}\log_{2}(p_{k}p_{k^{\prime}}q_{\ell|k,k^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2S_{i}(\Sigma)-\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\ell}p_{k}p_{k^{\prime}}q_{\ell|k,k^{\prime}}\log_{2}(q_{\ell|k,k^{\prime}})

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 2S_{i}(\Sigma)

Finally, notice that the subsequence
S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2^{k}})/2^{k}
is increasing, since

\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2^{k}})}{2^{k}}\geq\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2^{k-1}})}{2^{k}}+\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2^{k-1}})}{2^{k}}=\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2^{k-1}})}{2^{k-1}}

and the result follows since the
\limsup
of the whole sequence is greater than the
\limsup
of any of its subequence

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes N})}{N}\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2^{k}})}{2^{k}}\geq\frac{S_{i}(\Sigma^{\boxtimes 2})}{2}>S_{i}(\Sigma)

.
∎

Proof of proposition
4.2
.

BCT is a classical theory, therefore we immediately have that
S_{i}(\rho)=H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})
for any
i=1,2,3
(see proposition
4.1
). For
S^{\rm reg}_{i}
, we just notice that
S_{i}(\rho^{\boxtimes N})
is the Shannon of the factorized joint distribution
p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}},\mathsf{\bf{s}}}=p_{\mathbf{i}}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}
where
p_{\mathsf{\bf{i}}}=p_{i_{1}}\dots p_{i_{N}}
, thus

\frac{S_{i}(\rho^{\boxtimes N})}{N}=\frac{NH(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+(N-1)H(\frac{1}{2})}{N}=H(\mathsf{\bf{p}})+1-\frac{1}{N}

and the result follows by taking the limit.
∎
